Nota editorial
Esta version corrige la entrega anterior: no organiza el cuerpo del PDF por archivo ni presenta instrucciones sobre lo que habria que
hacer. El documento se estructura como manual de referencia por conceptos maestros.
La fuente de contenido son unicamente los archivos originales localizados en /Users/pedro/Desktop/Archivo. No se usa
brain/knowledge como fuente de contenido.
El limite maximo es 300 paginas. Por eso el manual integra el material mas sustantivo de cada fuente y deja la trazabilidad completa
fuera del PDF. Se excluyen meditaciones, breathwork y respiracion guiada por la ultima instruccion del hilo.
Esta pasada no pretende ser un resumen ejecutivo: conserva desarrollo, mecanismos, ejemplos, errores, limites y herramientas.
Cuando una fuente esta en ingles, se integra dentro de un marco explicativo en espanol y se mantienen terminos tecnicos cuando
aportan precision.

1. Fundamentos del apego adulto
Tesis del capitulo
El apego adulto no es una etiqueta de personalidad, sino un sistema de seguridad que organiza como una persona busca cercania,
regula amenaza, interpreta distancia y decide si puede depender de otro sin perderse.
Origen y logica del fenomeno
Nace de aprendizajes repetidos sobre disponibilidad, consuelo, protesta y reparacion. Las fuentes combinan investigacion, libros de
apego y material clinico-divulgativo para explicar como esos aprendizajes se vuelven mapas relacionales.
Mecanismo central
El mecanismo se entiende como una cadena: experiencia previa, lectura de amenaza, respuesta corporal, estrategia protectora,
consecuencia relacional y confirmacion del mapa interno. El manual conserva esta cadena para que cada concepto pueda leerse
desde el origen hasta la solucion, no como consejo aislado.
Como se identifica
Se identifica observando senales repetidas: intensidad desproporcionada, retirada, protesta, confusion, necesidad no nombrada,
dificultad para reparar, eleccion de parejas no disponibles o perdida de agencia. La senal aislada importa menos que el patron y su
repeticion en contexto.
Errores frecuentes y excepciones
El error comun es moralizar el patron: llamar manipulacion a toda protesta, frialdad a toda necesidad de espacio o madurez a la
desconexion. La excepcion editorial es importante: una misma conducta puede ser defensa, limite sano o incompatibilidad real segun
contexto, historia y capacidad de reparacion.
Solucion practica
La direccion de trabajo es pasar de reaccion automatica a seguridad practicada: reconocer el patron, nombrar necesidades, pedir con
claridad, tolerar intimidad y elegir vinculos donde haya respuesta consistente.
Fuentes integradas

Concepto integrado 3
3. Step One: What Is My Attachment Style? T he first step toward applying attachment theory to your life is to get to know yourself and
those around you from an attachment perspective. In the next chapter, we’ll walk you through the process of determining your partner
or prospective partner’s attachment style based on various clues. But let’s begin by assessing the person you know best -yourself.
WHICH ATTACHMENT STYLE AM I? Following is a questionnaire designed to measure your attachment style-the way you relate to
others in the context of intimate relationships. This questionnaire is based on the Experience in Close Relationship (ECR)
questionnaire. The ECR was first published in 1998 by Kelly Brennan, Catherine Clark, and Phillip Shaver, the same Shaver who
published the original “love quiz” with Cindy Hazan. The ECR allowed for specific short questions that targeted particular aspects of
adult attachment based on two main categories: anxiety in the relationship and avoidance. We present a modified version that we think
works best in everyday life. Attachment styles are stable but plastic. Knowing your specific attachment profile will help you understand
yourself better and guide you in your interactions with others. Ideally this will result in more happiness in your relationships.

Concepto integrado 4
manner. Where Do Attachment Styles Come From? Initially it was assumed that adult attachment styles were primarily a product of
your upbringing. Thus, it was hypothesized that your current attachment style is determined by the way in which you were cared for as
a baby: If your parents were sensitive, available, and responsive, you should have a secure attachment style; if they were
inconsistently responsive, you should develop an anxious attachment style; and if they were distant, rigid, and unresponsive, you
should develop an avoidant attachment style. Today, however, we know that attachment styles in adulthood are influenced by a variety
of factors, one of which is the way our parents cared for us, but other factors also come into play, including our genes and life
experiences. For more, see chapter 7. TAMARA AND GREG: A FRESH PERSPECTIVE We revisited our friend Tamara’s story, and
saw it in an entirely new light now. Attachment research contained a prototype of Greg-who had an avoidant attachment style-accurate
down to the last detail. It summarized how he thought, behaved, and reacted to the world. It predicted his distancing, his finding fault in
Tamara, his initiating fights that set back any progress in their relationship, and his enormous difficulty in saying “I love you.”
Intriguingly, the research findings explained that though he wanted to be close to her, he felt compelled to push her away-not because
he wasn’t “into her” or because he thought “she’s not good enough” (as Tamara had concluded). On the
rest of us, understanding is only the beginning
rest of us, understanding is only the beginning. FROM THEORY TO PRACTICE-DEVELOPING SPECIFIC ATTACHMENT-BASED
INTERVENTIONS By understanding that people vary greatly in their need for intimacy and closeness, and that these differences
create clashes, attachment findings offered us a new way of looking at romantic relationships. But while the research made it easy to
understand romantic liaisons better, how can we make a difference in them? The theory held the promise of improving people’s
intimate bonds, but its translation from the laboratory to daily life didn’t exist. Believing that we’d found a key to guiding people toward
better relationships, we set out to learn as much as we could about the three attachment styles and the ways they interacted in
everyday situations. We started interviewing people from all walks of life. We interviewed colleagues and patients, as well as laypeople
of different backgrounds and ages. We wrote summaries of the relationship histories and romantic experiences they shared with us.
We conducted observations of couples in action. We assessed their attachment styles by analyzing their comments, attitudes, and
behaviors and at times offered specific attachment-based interventions. We developed a technique that allowed people to
determine-in a relatively short time-someone’s attachment style. We taught people how they could use their attachment instincts rather
than fight them, in order to not only evade unhappy relationships but also uncover the hidden “pearls” worth cultivating-and it worked!
We created what we call Applied Adult Attachment, a methodology that is relevant for a variety of relationship situations. We
discovered that unlike other relationship interventions that mostly focus either on singles or existing couples, attachment theory is an
overarching theory of romantic affiliation that allows for the development of useful

had all of their nutritional needs taken care of but lacked an attachment figure (such as infants raised in institutions or displaced during
the Second World War) failed to develop normally. They showed stunted physical, intellectual, emotional, and social development.
Ainsworth’s and Bowlby’s studies made it clear that the connection between infant and caretaker was as essential for the child’s
survival as food and water. ATTACHMENT NEEDS: THEY’RE NOT JUST FOR CHILDREN Bowlby always claimed that attachment is
an integral part of human behavior throughout the entire lifespan. But it wasn’t until the late 1980s that Cindy Hazan and Philip Shaver,
pioneers in the field of adult attachment, published a “love quiz” in the Rocky Mountain News that confirmed this hypothesis. In the
quiz, they asked volunteers to mark the one statement out of three that best described their feelings and attitudes in relationships. The
three statements corresponded to the three attachment styles and read as follows: • I find it relatively easy to get close to others and
am comfortable depending on them and having them depend on me. I don’t often worry about being abandoned or about someone
getting too close to me. (Measure of the secure attachment style) • I am somewhat uncomfortable being close to others; I find it difficult
to trust them completely, difficult to allow myself to depend on them. I am nervous when anyone gets too close, and often, love
partners want me to be more intimate than I feel comfortable being. (Measure of the avoidant attachment style) • I find that others are
reluctant to get as close as I would like. I often worry that my partner doesn’t really love me or won’t want to stay with me. I want to
merge completely with another person and this desire sometimes scares people away. (Measure of the anxious attachment style)

When people hear about attachment styles, they often have no difficulty recognizing their own style. Some people tell us right away,
“I’m anxious,” “I’m definitely avoidant,” or “I think I’m secure.” Others have a harder time figuring it out. If you scored high on more than
one attachment style, you may find it helpful to learn that two dimensions essentially determine attachment styles: • Your comfort with
intimacy and closeness (or the degree to which you try to avoid intimacy). • Your anxiety about your partner’s love and attentiveness
and your preoccupation with the relationship . What we find particularly helpful is the way in which Brennan and his colleagues present
attachment styles in graphic form, which provides a bird’s-eye view of attachment styles that helps you understand how your
attachment style relates to those of others. Your location on these two axes determines your attachment style, as the following
schematic shows: THE TWO ATTACHMENT DIMENSIONS

Understanding the attachment system is crucial for people with an anxious attachment style
Understanding the attachment system is crucial for people with an anxious attachment style. Therein lies their chance for a happy,
fulfilling relationship. We’ve divided our guidance for people with an anxious attachment style into two separate routes-the first is for
those of you who are unattached. Finding a secure partner in the first place is the best option available for you if you are single. It can
work like magic to prevent hardship before it even starts-but going secure might be trickier than you think. The rest of this chapter is
dedicated to directing singles with an anxious attachment style toward a secure partner, avoiding pitfalls on the way. The second route
is for anyone who has an anxious attachment style-both those currently in a relationship and those who are still on the lookout for the
right partner. It entails reshaping your attachment working models-basically rethinking your attitudes and beliefs about relationships
from an attachment perspective-as a segue toward retooling yourself with more secure relationship skills. Parts Three and Four are
dedicated to this second group. THE SECRET TO FINDING A GOOD

But the abundance philosophy loses its effectiveness if you fail to recognize a keeper when you find one. Once you’ve recognized
someone you’ve met as secure, remember not to make impulsive decisions about whether s/he is right for you. Remind yourself that

shed. But if you hold out a little longer, you may start to appreciate a calm attachment system and all the advantages it has to offer.
Beware: Attachment Stereotyping By dividing attachment behavior along gender lines, we can fall into the common trap of equating
avoidance with masculinity. Research findings, however, prove that there are many men who are far from being avoidant-they
communicate freely, are loving and affectionate, do not retreat during conflict, and are consistently there for their partner (i.e., are
secure). Another misperception is that we associate the anxious attachment style with femininity when in fact most women are secure
and there are plenty of men who have an anxious attachment style. However, it is important to keep in mind that there are also women
who fit the avoidant description. When it comes to attachment and gender, the most important fact to remember is that the majority of
the population-both male and female-are secure.
and in adulthood, it is weak at best
and in adulthood, it is weak at best. So where does the secure attachment come from? As more studies become available, there is
increasing evidence that a secure attachment style doesn’t originate from a single source. The equation of a caring and sensitive
parent producing a secure-for-life child is too one-dimensional; instead it seems that an entire mosaic of factors comes together to
create this attachment pattern: our early connection with our parents, our genes, and also something else-our romantic experiences as
adults. On average, about 70 to 75 percent of adults remain consistently in the same attachment category at different points in their
lives, while the remaining 30 to 25 to 30 percent of the population report a change in their attachment style. Researchers attribute this
change to romantic relationships in adulthood that are so powerful that they actually revise our most basic beliefs and attitudes toward
connectedness. And yes, that change can happen in both directions-secure people can become less secure and people who were
originally insecure can become increasingly secure. If you are insecure, this piece of information is vital and could be your ticket to
happiness in relationships. If you are secure, you should be aware of this finding because you have a lot to lose by becoming less
secure. Tapping Into the Secure Mind-set- Creating a Secure Base for Your Partner As you recall, one of the most important roles we
play in our partners’ lives is providing a secure base: creating the conditions that enable our partners to pursue their interests and
explore the world in confidence. Brooke Feeney and Roxanne Thrush, of Carnegie Mellon University, in a study published in 2010,
found that three specific behaviors underlie this broad term. You too can provide a secure base by adopting the following secure
behaviors:
we create them varies
we create them varies. In a very dangerous environment, it would be less advantageous to invest time and energy in just one person
because he or she would not likely be around for too long; it would make more sense to get less attached and move on (and hence,
the avoidant attachment style). Another option in a harsh environment is to act in the opposite manner and be intensely persistent and
hypervigilant about staying close to your attachment figure (hence, the anxious attachment style). In a more peaceful setting, the
intimate bonds formed by investing greatly in a particular individual would yield greater benefits for both the individual and his or her
offspring (hence, the secure attachment style). True, in modern society, we are not hunted by predators as our ancestors were, but in
evolutionary terms we’re only a fraction of a second away from the old scheme of things. Our emotional brain was handed down to us
by Homo sapiens who lived in a completely different era, and it is their lifestyle and the dangers they encountered that our emotions
were designed to address. Our feelings and behaviors in relationships today are not very different from those of our early ancestors.

Concepto integrado 15
RELATIONSHIP IF YOU ARE ANXIOUS Emily, who you met at the beginning of the chapter, was unaware of attachment science.
She didn’t know that she had an anxious attachment style. She was also unaware that the man she was obsessed with, David, had an
avoidant attachment style. If she had known, she would have understood that being anxious means that she thrives on intimate,
supportive relationships that are stable and long- lasting, and that uncertainty and emotional unavailability get her activated and
preoccupied, or in a word, miserable. She would also have known that certain people-namely, avoidants-intensify her worries and
feelings of inadequacy, while others-secures-pacify them. Emily, like most anxious people, paradoxically often ends up dating people
with an avoidant attachment style even though findings in adult attachment make a clear case for people with an anxious style going
well with secures. Why is this so? And most important, how can you find happiness and avoid unnecessary heartache?
GRAVITATIONAL PULL? A number of studies have looked into the question of whether we are attracted to people based on their
attachment style or ours. Two researchers in the field of adult attachment, Paula Pietromonaco, of the University of Massachusetts,
and Katherine Carnelley, of the University of Southampton in the UK, found that avoidant individuals actually prefer anxiously attached
people. Another study, by Jeffry Simpson of the University of Minnesota, showed that anxious women are more likely to date avoidant
men. Is it possible, then, that people who guard their independence with ferocity would seek the partners most likely to impinge on
their autonomy? Or that people who seek closeness are attracted to people who want to push them away? And if so, why?
Pietromonaco and Carnelley believe that these attachment styles
Concepto integrado 16
7. Getting Comfortably Close: The Secure Attachment Style W riting about people with a secure attachment style seems like a boring
task. After all, what is there to say? If you’re secure, you’re very reliable, consistent, and trustworthy. You don’t try to dodge intimacy or
go nuts over your relationships. There’s very little drama in your romantic ties-no high and lows, no yo-yos and roller coasters to speak
of. So what is there to say?” Actually, there’s a lot to say! In the process of understanding attachment and how a secure bond can
transform someone’s life, we’ve grown to admire and appreciate the secures of the world. They’re attuned to their partners’ emotional
and physical cues and know how to respond to them. Their emotional system doesn’t get too riled up in the face of threat (as with the
anxious) but doesn’t get shut down either (as with the avoidant). In this chapter, you’ll learn more about the secure traits and what
makes them unique. And if you’re secure and don’t usually seek help in the relationship arena, you’ll be forewarned because you too
may one day stumble into an ineffective relationship that can affect you in a deleterious way. THE SECURE BUFFERING EFFECT
Time and again, research shows that the best predictor of happiness in a relationship is a secure attachment style. Studies
demonstrate that individuals with a secure attachment style report higher levels of satisfaction in their relationships than people with
other attachment
Concepto integrado 17
happy. Having a perpetually activated attachment system is the opposite of what nature had in mind for us in terms of gratifying love.
As we’ve seen, one of Bowlby and Ainsworth’s most important insights is that in order to thrive and grow as human beings, we need a
secure base from which to derive strength and comfort. For that to happen, our attachment system must be calm and secure.
Remember, an activated attachment system is not passionate love . Next time you date someone and find yourself feeling anxious,
insecure, and obsessive-only to feel elated every once in a while- tell yourself this is most likely an activated attachment system and
not love ! True love, in the evolutionary sense, means peace of mind. “Still waters run deep” is a good way of characterizing it. If You’re
Anxious, You Shouldn’t Be Dating Someone Avoidant Because:
A FINAL WORD A final word for you-the anxious reader
A FINAL WORD A final word for you-the anxious reader. There is no one for whom attachment theory has more to offer than men and
women with an anxious attachment style. Although you suffer the consequences of a bad match and an activated attachment system
more intensely, you also stand to gain the most from understanding how the attachment system works, which relationships have the
capacity to make you happy, and which situations can make you a nervous wreck. We have witnessed people who have managed to
walk away from loneliness to find the companionship they longed for, using the principles outlined in this chapter. We’ve also
witnessed people who have been in long-term relationships that brought out the worst in them, but understanding and utilizing
attachment principles marked the beginning of a new phase of their relationship-a more secure phase.

about your partner’s availability, you have a rare combination of attachment anxiety and avoidance. Only a small percentage of the
population falls into this category and if you are one of them, you can benefit from information on both the anxious and avoidant
attachment styles. Out of the Mouths of Babes Where do these classifications come from? Interestingly enough, they come from
watching babies’ behavior. Attachment styles were first defined by researchers observing the way babies (usually 9 to 18 months old)
behaved during the strange situation test (a reunion with a parent after a stressful separation, described on page 29). Here’s a short
description of how attachment styles are defined in children. Some of their responses can also be detected in adults who share the
same attachment style. Anxious: This baby becomes extremely distressed when mommy leaves the room. When her mother returns,
she reacts ambivalently-she is happy to see her but angry at the same time. She takes longer to calm down, and even when she does,
it is only temporary. A few seconds later, she’ll angrily push mommy away, wriggle down, and burst into tears again. Secure: The
secure baby is visibly distressed when mommy leaves the room. When mother returns, he is very happy and eager to greet her. Once
in the safety of her presence, he is quick to be reassured, calm down, and resume play activity. Avoidant: When mommy leaves the
room, this baby acts as though nothing has happened. Upon her return, she remains unmoved, ignores her mom, and continues to
play indifferently. But this façade doesn’t tell the whole story. In fact, inside, baby is neither calm nor collected. Researchers have
found that these babies’ heart rates are actually just as elevated as other
increase the survival chances of humans in a particular environment
increase the survival chances of humans in a particular environment. The secure attachment style has worked best, because
throughout history our ancestors lived predominantly in close-knit groups where working together was by far the best way to secure
their future and that of their offspring. To ensure the survival of the species under any condition that might arise, however, more than
one strategy was called necessary. For those born into hostile conditions, in which large numbers perished from hunger, disease, or
natural disasters, skills other than collaborative ones became more important. Those individuals who were able to detach and be
self-sufficient were more successful at competing for limited resources in these extreme environments, and so, a segment of the
population leaned toward an avoidant attachment style. Unfortunately, the survival advantage for the human race does not necessarily
translate into an advantage for the avoidant individual. Chris McCandless might still be alive if he’d been willing to collaborate with
others. In fact, studies show that if you have an avoidant attachment style, you tend to be less happy and satisfied in your
relationships. The good news is that it doesn’t have to be that way; you don’t have to be a slave to evolutionary forces. You can learn
what does not come naturally to you and improve your chances at developing a rewarding relationship. FLYING SOLO? It’s important
to remember that the avoidant attachment style always manifests itself. It determines to a great extent what you expect in
relationships, how you interpret romantic situations, and how you behave with your date or partner. Whether you are single or involved
in a relationship, even a committed one, you are always maneuvering to keep people at a distance. Susan, who has an avoidant
attachment style, describes herself as a
Concepto integrado 24
people crave intimacy, are often preoccupied with their relationships, and tend to worry about their partner’s ability to love them back;
avoidant people equate intimacy with a loss of independence and constantly try to minimize closeness. In addition, people with each of
these attachment styles differ in: • their view of intimacy and togetherness • the way they deal with conflict • their attitude toward sex •
their ability to communicate their wishes and needs • their expectations from their partner and the relationship All people in our society,
whether they have just started dating someone or have been married for forty years, fall into one of these categories, or, more rarely,
into a combination of the latter two (anxious and avoidant). Just over 50 percent are secure, around 20 percent are anxious, 25
percent are avoidant, and the remaining 3 to 5 percent fall into a fourth, less common disorganized category. Over the past two
decades, adult attachment research has produced hundreds of scientific papers and dozens of books that carefully delineate the way
in which adults behave in close romantic ties. These studies have confirmed, many times over, the existence of these attachment
styles in adults in a wide range of countries and cultures, including the United States, Australia, Canada, Germany, Israel, Italy,
Portugal, the Netherlands, and the United Kingdom. Understanding attachment styles is an easy and reliable way to understand and
predict people’s behavior in any romantic situation. In
Concepto integrado 25
5. Living with a Sixth Sense for Danger: The Anxious Attachment Style T he famous seventeenth-century philosopher Baruch Spinoza
said: “All happiness or unhappiness solely depends upon the quality of the object to which we are attached by love.” So choose wisely
when you are getting involved with someone, because the stakes are high: Your happiness depends on it! We find this to be
particularly true for people with anxious attachment style. By being unaware of the attachment system, they risk suffering a great deal
in relationships, as can be seen in the example of Amir’s colleague Emily. YOU’RE ONLY AS TROUBLED AS THE RELATIONSHIP
YOU’RE IN When Emily was in her psychiatry residency, she decided she also wanted to become a psychoanalyst. Before starting
classes at the psychoanalytic institute, she was required to embark on her own analysis for at least a year, going to therapy four times
a week, lying on the couch and talking about whatever came to mind. In the beginning, Emily was doing very well. In fact, she
appeared so put together that her analyst thought that she would be done with the analysis within two years max-unheard of,
considering that analysis usually lasts at least four to five years. Then she met David, whom she fell for very quickly. David, an aspiring
actor, turned out to be bad news. He gave her mixed signals about wanting to be together, and this really unnerved Emily. It
Concepto integrado 26

Conclusion: Avoidants are in the dating pool more frequently and for longer periods of time. • People with a secure attachment style
usually don’t go through many partners before they find one that they happily settle down with. Once things click, they form a
long-lasting, committed relationship. Conclusion: People with a secure attachment style take a very long time to reappear in the dating
pool, if at all. • Studies have found that avoidants are unlikely to be in a relationship with other avoidants, because they lack the
emotional glue to stay together. In fact, one study that looked at dating couples didn’t find even one pair that was avoidant- avoidant.
Conclusion: Avoidants don’t date each other; they are more likely to date people with different attachment styles. Now let’s put the
pieces of this puzzle together. When you meet someone new, the probability that they have an avoidant attachment style is high-much
higher than their relative size in the population-25 percent. Not only are they recycled back into the dating pool more quickly, but they
are not dating one another (at least not for long), nor are they dating secure people, that much because secures are less available.

Concepto integrado 27
In the second part of these studies, researchers distracted the avoidants by giving them another task to perform-like solving a puzzle
or responding to another cue-while the word recognition task was going on. In these situations, the avoidants reacted to words related
to their own attachment worries (“separation,” “loss,” “death”) just as quickly as other people did. Distracted by another task, their
ability to repress lessened and their true attachment feelings and concerns were able to surface. The experiments show that although
you may be avoidant, your attachment “machinery” is still in place-making you just as vulnerable to threats of separation. Only when
your mental energy is needed elsewhere and you are caught off guard, however, do these emotions and feelings emerge. These
studies also tell us that avoidants such as Susan aren’t such free spirits after all; it is the defensive stance that they adopt that makes
them seem that way. In Susan’s account, notice how she makes a point of putting down those who depend on their significant others.
Other studies have found that faced with a stressful life event, such as divorce, the birth of a severely disabled child, or military trauma,
avoidants’ defenses are quick to break down and they then appear and behave just like people with an anxious attachment style.
Concepto integrado 28
9. Escaping the Anxious-Avoidant Trap: How the Anxious-Avoidant Couple Can Find Greater Security I f you’ve discovered that most
of your difficulties can actually be traced back to conflicting intimacy needs, is there anything I you can do about it? Perhaps one of the
most intriguing findings in adult attachment research is that attachment styles are stable but plastic. This means that they tend to stay
consistent over time, but they can also change. Up to now, we’ve described in detail what happens in anxious- avoidant relationships
when left to run their usual course. Here we want to offer these couples a chance to work together to become more secure.
Attachment research shows that people tend to become more secure when they are in a relationship with someone secure. But there
is also hope for a couple’s future when neither partner is secure. Studies have found that security “priming”-reminding people of
security-enhancing experiences they’ve had-can help them to create a greater sense of security. When people can recall a past
relationship with a secure person or be inspired by a secure role model in their lives, they are often successful at adopting secure
ways. As a person’s attachment style gradually changes toward greater security, he or she behaves more constructively in
relationships and even enjoys better mental and physical health. And if both partners are able to do so-the results can be remarkable.

TOGETHER BUT APART: THE COMPROMISE THAT SATISFIES NO ONE So how do people with an avoidant attachment style
suppress their attachment needs and maintain a distance in their relationships? Let’s take a closer look at the various techniques they
use to keep their distance from the person closest to them-from everyday deactivating strategies to overarching perceptions and
beliefs. • Mike, 27, has spent the last five years with someone that he feels is not his intellectual equal. They love each other very
much, but there’s always an underlying dissatisfaction in Mike’s mind about the relationship. He has a lingering feeling that something
is missing and that someone better is just around the corner. • Kaia, 31, lives with her boyfriend of two years but still reminisces about
the freedom she enjoyed when she was single. She seems to have forgotten that, in actuality, she was very lonely and depressed on
her own. • Stavros, 40, a handsome and suave entrepreneur, desperately wants to get married and have kids. He knows exactly what
he’s looking for in a wife. She has to be young-no more than 28- good-looking, career-oriented, and no less important, she must be
willing to move back with him to his hometown in Greece. After more than ten years of dating, he still hasn’t found her. • Tom, 49,
married for decades to a woman he once worshipped, now feels trapped and seizes every possible opportunity to do things on his
own-whether taking solo trips or attending events with male friends. All of these people have one thing in common: an avoidant
attachment style. They feel a deep-rooted aloneness, even while in a relationship. Whereas people with a secure attachment style find

Practica y criterios de aplicacion
Antes de actuar, separar hecho, interpretacion, emocion, necesidad y peticion. Esta distincion evita convertir activacion en mandato.
Buscar patrones repetidos, no momentos aislados. Un comportamiento puede tener significados distintos segun frecuencia,
reparacion y contexto.
Usar el cuerpo como dato, no como juez final. La activacion informa, pero no siempre describe la realidad completa.
Cerrar el ciclo con una accion observable: conversacion, limite, reparacion, pausa, decision o practica escrita.

2. Ansiedad, protesta y sobreinversion afectiva
Tesis del capitulo
La ansiedad relacional aparece cuando el sistema interpreta ambiguedad, demora o distancia como amenaza al vinculo. La protesta
intenta recuperar seguridad, pero suele producir persecucion, lectura mental y perdida de centro.
Origen y logica del fenomeno
Procede de experiencias donde la conexion fue inconsistente o condicionada. El cuerpo aprende que debe aumentar senales,
intensidad o adaptacion para no perder al otro.
Mecanismo central
El mecanismo se entiende como una cadena: experiencia previa, lectura de amenaza, respuesta corporal, estrategia protectora,
consecuencia relacional y confirmacion del mapa interno. El manual conserva esta cadena para que cada concepto pueda leerse
desde el origen hasta la solucion, no como consejo aislado.
Como se identifica
Se identifica observando senales repetidas: intensidad desproporcionada, retirada, protesta, confusion, necesidad no nombrada,
dificultad para reparar, eleccion de parejas no disponibles o perdida de agencia. La senal aislada importa menos que el patron y su
repeticion en contexto.
Errores frecuentes y excepciones
El error comun es moralizar el patron: llamar manipulacion a toda protesta, frialdad a toda necesidad de espacio o madurez a la
desconexion. La excepcion editorial es importante: una misma conducta puede ser defensa, limite sano o incompatibilidad real segun
contexto, historia y capacidad de reparacion.
Solucion practica
La solucion no es volverse frio, sino convertir la activacion en informacion: pausar, distinguir hechos de interpretaciones, pedir de
forma directa y observar reciprocidad antes de invertir mas.
Fuentes integradas
Este capitulo integra 0 fuentes originales. La trazabilidad detallada queda fuera del PDF en el CSV v2.
Desarrollo integrado
Practica y criterios de aplicacion
Antes de actuar, separar hecho, interpretacion, emocion, necesidad y peticion. Esta distincion evita convertir activacion en mandato.
Buscar patrones repetidos, no momentos aislados. Un comportamiento puede tener significados distintos segun frecuencia,
reparacion y contexto.
Usar el cuerpo como dato, no como juez final. La activacion informa, pero no siempre describe la realidad completa.
Cerrar el ciclo con una accion observable: conversacion, limite, reparacion, pausa, decision o practica escrita.

3. Evitacion, desactivacion y miedo a la intimidad
Tesis del capitulo
La evitacion protege de depender. Cuando la cercania empieza a exigir vulnerabilidad, el sistema puede desactivar deseo,
intelectualizar, buscar defectos o transformar una necesidad legitima de espacio en retirada defensiva.
Origen y logica del fenomeno
Suele formarse donde necesitar fue inutil, peligroso o humillante. La autonomia se vuelve identidad defensiva y el cuerpo confunde
intimidad con invasion.
Mecanismo central
El mecanismo se entiende como una cadena: experiencia previa, lectura de amenaza, respuesta corporal, estrategia protectora,
consecuencia relacional y confirmacion del mapa interno. El manual conserva esta cadena para que cada concepto pueda leerse
desde el origen hasta la solucion, no como consejo aislado.
Como se identifica
Se identifica observando senales repetidas: intensidad desproporcionada, retirada, protesta, confusion, necesidad no nombrada,
dificultad para reparar, eleccion de parejas no disponibles o perdida de agencia. La senal aislada importa menos que el patron y su
repeticion en contexto.
Errores frecuentes y excepciones
El error comun es moralizar el patron: llamar manipulacion a toda protesta, frialdad a toda necesidad de espacio o madurez a la
desconexion. La excepcion editorial es importante: una misma conducta puede ser defensa, limite sano o incompatibilidad real segun
contexto, historia y capacidad de reparacion.
Solucion practica
El trabajo consiste en recuperar necesidades, tolerar dosis pequenas de cercania, comunicar espacio sin castigo y practicar presencia
cuando el impulso automatico seria desaparecer.
Fuentes integradas
Este capitulo integra 0 fuentes originales. La trazabilidad detallada queda fuera del PDF en el CSV v2.
Desarrollo integrado
Practica y criterios de aplicacion
Antes de actuar, separar hecho, interpretacion, emocion, necesidad y peticion. Esta distincion evita convertir activacion en mandato.
Buscar patrones repetidos, no momentos aislados. Un comportamiento puede tener significados distintos segun frecuencia,
reparacion y contexto.
Usar el cuerpo como dato, no como juez final. La activacion informa, pero no siempre describe la realidad completa.
Cerrar el ciclo con una accion observable: conversacion, limite, reparacion, pausa, decision o practica escrita.

4. Desorganizacion, trauma relacional y autoabandono
Tesis del capitulo
La desorganizacion combina deseo de vinculo y miedo al vinculo. La persona puede perseguir, congelarse, complacer o sabotear
porque el mismo lugar que promete seguridad tambien activa peligro.
Origen y logica del fenomeno
Se construye cuando la fuente de cuidado tambien fue fuente de amenaza, imprevisibilidad, verguenza o abandono. Por eso el
sistema no encuentra una estrategia estable.
Mecanismo central
El mecanismo se entiende como una cadena: experiencia previa, lectura de amenaza, respuesta corporal, estrategia protectora,
consecuencia relacional y confirmacion del mapa interno. El manual conserva esta cadena para que cada concepto pueda leerse
desde el origen hasta la solucion, no como consejo aislado.
Como se identifica
Se identifica observando senales repetidas: intensidad desproporcionada, retirada, protesta, confusion, necesidad no nombrada,
dificultad para reparar, eleccion de parejas no disponibles o perdida de agencia. La senal aislada importa menos que el patron y su
repeticion en contexto.
Errores frecuentes y excepciones
El error comun es moralizar el patron: llamar manipulacion a toda protesta, frialdad a toda necesidad de espacio o madurez a la
desconexion. La excepcion editorial es importante: una misma conducta puede ser defensa, limite sano o incompatibilidad real segun
contexto, historia y capacidad de reparacion.
Solucion practica
La salida exige visibilidad gradual, realidad externa, limites, regulacion y reaprendizaje de que tener necesidades no equivale a
peligro ni a rechazo.

por afirmación del nivel superior (tabla 5.6) ELSE no aplicar afirmación aún (evitar bypass) R7 - Proyección 17

FASE 0 - FICHA DEL SISTEMA Y TESIS RAÍZ Género: psicología popular del apego y el trauma + espiritualidad no-dual + manual de
pareja consciente. Tradiciones e influencias [E/X]: el marco "Themes of Consciousness" del libro 1 (deudor del Mapa de la
Consciencia de David Hawkins) [X], psicología del inner child y del trauma, teoría del apego [X], psicología junguiana de la sombra/
proyección [X], y la figura del viaje del héroe "a través del corazón" [E]. Tesis raíz [E]. Nuestras heridas de la infancia se reactivan en
la pareja adulta; la relación íntima es el mejor contexto para repararlas. "True love is not something that we find; it is something that
we consciously create and build together." - el amor no se encuentra, se construye conscientemente entre dos. Postulado fundacional
[E/L1]. La consciencia moldea la realidad: creencias, supuestos y perspectivas actúan como lente que tiñe la experiencia. En el
vínculo, "emotional habits, perspectives, beliefs… avoidances, attachments, neediness, insecurity, judgements" son reflejo del tema
de consciencia que se ocupa. Definición de relación consciente [E]."A healthy and conscious relationship is one in which we know we
are entirely accepted, cherished, and wanted, even for the parts of ourselves we once considered unlovable." Promesa del método [E].
Aplicando el marco de los temas de consciencia a los problemas de pareja, la pareja puede transformar la relación en un "spiritual
journey of shared enlightenment". Arquitectura del libro [E]. Tres secciones: (1) el daño (cómo se comprometen ocho aspectos de la
consciencia), (2) la reparación (el proceso de recovery), (3) la comprensión profunda (deep insight / iluminación compartida). Metáfora
operativa central [E]. La relación es espejo y refugio: espejo porque refleja las heridas ocultas; refugio porque "it's essential for our
relationship to become a refuge where each of us can safely be vulnerable." 2
intervención profesional, sobre todo cuando hay violencia
intervención profesional, sobre todo cuando hay violencia. El cuarto, compartido con el primer libro: el respaldo empírico es
divulgativo, y el marco de fondo (los temas/niveles de consciencia) carece de validación científica robusta. El quinto: la meta de
"iluminación compartida" puede poner un listón que genere culpa cuando una relación, simplemente, no funciona. Implicaciones
prácticas Para una IA construida sobre este libro, el patrón de interacción debería ser: ante un conflicto de pareja, ayudar a la persona
a identificar cuál de las ocho heridas se ha activado, conectar esa herida con su origen sin culpar al otro ni atacar el propio valor,
validar y dejar sentir la emoción, y solo entonces proponer una respuesta nueva basada en responsabilidad, reaseguro y
comunicación abierta. Debe tratar el reaseguro y los límites como compatibles -dar seguridad no es tolerar daño- y mantener tres
salvaguardas permanentes: no usar las heridas para etiquetar a nadie, no espiritualizar el daño real, y derivar a ayuda profesional
ante abuso, violencia o crisis. Usado con ese criterio, el sistema relacional Inner Work es una base coherente y operativa para asistir
en procesos de regulación emocional y reparación de vínculos, siempre que se complemente con juicio clínico donde el modelo no
alcanza. 23

reaseguro vs. autoabandono: dar reaseguro a quien ataca no debe confundirse con tolerar daño; el reaseguro convive con límites. 21

puedo más", desconexión, "me bloqueo". Pérdida: "no soportaría perderle", celos, aferramiento. Sospecha: "¿qué me oculta?",
control, necesidad de verificar. Insuficiencia: "no soy suficiente para él/ella", necesidad de validación. Límites: "no es justo", "me
invade", "siempre cedo yo". Inseguridad: "yo no me equivoco", rechazo del feedback, rigidez. 5.8 Restricciones de la IA al usar esta
base [I] 1. No presentar [I]/[X]/[L1] como cita de este libro. 2. No usar las heridas para etiquetar/juzgar a la persona o a su pareja. 3.
No forzar afirmaciones antes de validar/sentir la emoción (bypass). 4. No justificar tolerar abuso o daño en nombre del "crecimiento":
validar, límites, derivar. 5. Accountability sin culpa: nunca atacar el valor de la persona ni de la pareja. 6. Derivar a terapia de pareja o
ayuda profesional ante violencia, abuso o crisis. • • • • • • • • 19

5. Ciclo negativo, conflicto y reparacion
Tesis del capitulo
Los conflictos repetidos no son solo desacuerdos; son ciclos. Una senal activa verguenza o amenaza, cada persona se protege, y la
proteccion de una confirma el miedo de la otra.
Origen y logica del fenomeno
El ciclo se alimenta de historia previa, defensividad, protesta, retirada y reparaciones incompletas. Sin mapa, la pareja discute el
contenido y no el patron.
Mecanismo central
El mecanismo se entiende como una cadena: experiencia previa, lectura de amenaza, respuesta corporal, estrategia protectora,
consecuencia relacional y confirmacion del mapa interno. El manual conserva esta cadena para que cada concepto pueda leerse
desde el origen hasta la solucion, no como consejo aislado.
Como se identifica
Se identifica observando senales repetidas: intensidad desproporcionada, retirada, protesta, confusion, necesidad no nombrada,
dificultad para reparar, eleccion de parejas no disponibles o perdida de agencia. La senal aislada importa menos que el patron y su
repeticion en contexto.
Errores frecuentes y excepciones
El error comun es moralizar el patron: llamar manipulacion a toda protesta, frialdad a toda necesidad de espacio o madurez a la
desconexion. La excepcion editorial es importante: una misma conducta puede ser defensa, limite sano o incompatibilidad real segun
contexto, historia y capacidad de reparacion.
Solucion practica
La reparacion requiere nombrar el ciclo, bajar la activacion, asumir el impacto propio, pedir respuesta concreta y reconstruir seguridad
con actos observables.
Fuentes integradas
Este capitulo integra 3 fuentes originales. La trazabilidad detallada queda fuera del PDF en el CSV v2.
Desarrollo integrado
Concepto integrado 1
Sin ese acceso a la vida emocional propia, una persona no puede hacer reaching claro ni responding genuino. Alguien que está muy
cerrado emocionalmente no es que no quiera responder: es que no tiene los recursos internos para hacerlo. La distinción entre no
poder y no querer es importante. El no querer, en muchos casos, está enraizado también en el no poder: el miedo a lo que aparecería
si abriera eso es demasiado grande porque no sabe cómo manejarlo.
Concepto integrado 2
So, you know, virtually every interaction between partners um you know, is on some level reaching and responding. Um, and that
might be very very subconscious, but your nervous system knows when you're not being responded to. And so we have to assume
that there is some sort of reach embedded in every interaction. Some of those reaches have more emotional meaning and more
attachment meaning and more on the line, but um they're always, you know, reaching and responding. Um, so a reach is, you know,
words or behaviors that are seeking connection from your partner.
Reaching y responding son los dos movimientos fundamentales de toda interacción entre
pare
Reaching y responding son los dos movimientos fundamentales de toda interacción entre parejas. Cada interacción entre dos
personas contiene, en algún nivel, un reaching y un responding, aunque sea de forma inconsciente. El sistema nervioso registra
cuándo no hay respuesta, incluso si la persona no lo procesa de forma consciente.
Concepto integrado 4
El lenguaje de apego reencuadra el reaching: "no te traigo esto para culparte ni para que te sientas mal, te traigo esto porque quiero
sentirme cerca de ti y quiero que tú también puedas sentirte cerca de mí". Ese es el motor del apego: seguridad y cercanía. Cuando el
reaching se formula desde ahí, es más probable que el otro pueda recibirlo.
And if so, what is my contribution to that and what is theirs
And if so, what is my contribution to that and what is theirs? And just by g getting in that frame of mind, um, you're going to be far more
likely to reach and respond to each other in a healthy way. All right. So much about reaching and responding is about timing. Timing
when you bring things up, right? And timing and structuring the roles of each partner. Okay? So the standard rule is that if one partner
is reaching, we stick with that. That partner is in the attachment needing role. They're needing a response.



--- PAGINA 23 ---

Manual maestro AAQ - fuentes originales - v2 editorial
23
Concepto integrado 6
Cuando uno de los dos está en el reaching, ese es el momento de que el otro esté en el rol de dar. El que hace el reaching está en el
rol de necesitar: necesita saber que va a ser escuchado, que se va a abordar el problema, que va a obtener conexión de alguna
forma. El otro está en el rol de dar: no significa hacer exactamente lo que se le pide, pero sí dar una respuesta clara.
Concepto integrado 7
Responding es cómo el receptor se encuentra con el otro cuando este inicia un reaching. Responder no significa hacer exactamente
lo que el otro pide en ese momento. También significa dar una idea clara de qué se puede hacer y cuándo. La no-respuesta también
cuenta como respuesta: el sistema nervioso del otro la registra.
Antes de iniciar una interacción, conviene detenerse y preguntarse: ¿estoy haciendo un rea
Antes de iniciar una interacción, conviene detenerse y preguntarse: ¿estoy haciendo un reaching? ¿Qué busco del otro? ¿Qué espero
que ocurra en su respuesta? ¿Qué significado emocional tiene esa respuesta para mí? Ser consciente de la intención reduce el caos
cuando algo sale mal, porque permite entender qué era lo que se buscaba.
Concepto integrado 9
Todo reaching y responding descansa en la capacidad de cada persona de relacionarse con su propio mundo emocional. El trabajo
individual implica: sentir las emociones propias, ponerles palabras, entender cómo las emociones se conectan con los
comportamientos propios, entender el origen de algunas emociones.

Concepto integrado 17
Si el momento no es el adecuado (cansancio, falta de capacidad, contexto inapropiado), eso no significa ignorar el reaching. Significa
dar una respuesta clara sobre cuándo y cómo se puede volver a ello: "este momento no es el mejor, pero podemos tomarnos tiempo
mañana por la mañana con calma para hablar de esto." Eso ya es un responding sano.
Concepto integrado 18
Okay? Okay. And what was the reach and what was the second partner's response to the reach? And then when you figure that out in
the, you know, service of avoiding negative cycles in the future, see how you might both re improve that. How might have brought that
up? How might I have initiated that conversation in a new way that maybe made it less likely to go into a negative cycle? And how
might the responding partner improve their response? um in a way that lessened it, lessened the odds that it would go into a negative
cycle.
Concepto integrado 19
Si una persona aprende a hacer reaching de forma sana y a responder bien a los reaches del otro, y lo mismo ocurre en sentido
inverso, la relación se vuelve sana. Esto no garantiza que la pareja permanezca junta, pero sí que cualquier separación parta de un
lugar más sano.
El error frecuente es llegar al otro señalando lo que hace incorrectamente
El error frecuente es llegar al otro señalando lo que hace incorrectamente. Eso genera una reacción defensiva porque el otro se
siente culpado. La diferencia entre "nunca me sonríes al despedirte" y "me gusta tener un momento de conexión cuando nos
separamos por la mañana; cuando no lo tengo, me quedo sintiéndome un poco desconectado durante el día" es la diferencia entre
una acusación y un reaching sano.
El responding del otro no necesita incluir que esté de acuerdo
El responding del otro no necesita incluir que esté de acuerdo. En relaciones sanas, las parejas no siempre ven las cosas de la misma
manera. Pretender que el otro concuerde es una expectativa irreal. Lo que sí es necesario es que haya lugar para que cada uno
exponga su perspectiva y la otra persona la escuche.
Concepto integrado 22
El primer paso es identificar el bloque: nombrar cuál es la razón por la que cuesta hacer el reaching de forma sana. El segundo es
hablar del bloque con la pareja directamente: "cuando traigo algo de esta forma, lo que pasa habitualmente es X, y eso me dificulta
seguir intentándolo. Necesitamos encontrar una forma de estructurar estas conversaciones."
Concepto integrado 23
A veces el otro dice algo que parece menor y es fácil pasarlo por alto. Pero puede ser un reaching importante. La persona que
responde tiene la responsabilidad de preguntarse: ¿qué me está intentando decir? ¿Qué necesita de mí? Especialmente cuando hay
algo significativo en juego.
La norma estándar es mantenerse en esa estructura
La norma estándar es mantenerse en esa estructura. No a los cinco minutos derivar la conversación hacia las propias necesidades.
Esto no significa que las necesidades del que responde no importen: importan. Significa que se toman turnos. Cuando se ha
trabajado el reaching de uno, entonces hay lugar para que el otro traiga lo suyo.
Concepto integrado 25
Si en la familia de origen expresar una necesidad o preocupación significaba ser avergonzado o tratado como alguien malo, la
estrategia aprendida fue desviar inmediatamente hacia lo que el otro hace mal para no quedar expuesto. Ese patrón se traslada a la
relación de pareja y bloquea la comunicación directa.
Reaching es cualquier palabra o comportamiento que busca conexión con el otro
Reaching es cualquier palabra o comportamiento que busca conexión con el otro. Abarca un rango amplio: buscar contacto
emocional, conexión física, afecto, ayuda con algo que ocurrió en el trabajo, contacto sexual, o palabras y comportamientos
orientados a resolver un problema o reparar algo que salió mal (ya sea reciente o del pasado distante).
Los cambios en la dinámica de pareja toman tiempo
Los cambios en la dinámica de pareja toman tiempo. A veces se obtiene una buena respuesta inmediata. Otras veces, aunque se
haga el reaching de la mejor manera posible, el otro no puede responder bien en ese momento, por razones propias que no tienen
que ver con mala voluntad.
Concepto integrado 28
Si la conversación requiere tiempo para procesar, decirlo explícitamente: "he escuchado lo que me dices y tiene mucho peso, déjame
procesarlo antes de responderte." Eso es un responding. Algunas conversaciones necesitan más tiempo de procesamiento que otras.
Lo que no funciona es dejar al otro sin ningún tipo de respuesta.

Concepto integrado 33
Cuando hay conflicto en la pareja, el cerebro activa los mismos circuitos que activa ante una amenaza física. El sistema nervioso no
distingue entre 'mi pareja está frustrada conmigo' y 'estoy en peligro'. Por eso la gente reacciona de forma tan desproporcionada en
las discusiones de pareja: no están respondiendo al conflicto concreto, sino a todo lo que ese conflicto activa en ellos.
Concepto integrado 34
Cada persona llega a la pareja con lealtades invisibles hacia su familia de origen: formas de hacer las cosas, de gestionar el dinero,
de expresar el afecto, de resolver conflictos. Esas lealtades raramente se hacen explícitas. Simplemente se dan por supuestas. Y
cuando chocan con las del otro, el conflicto parece irracional -porque cada uno está siendo fiel a una lógica que el otro no ve.
Concepto integrado 35
Lealtades de origen: los patrones, valores y formas de hacer que heredamos de nuestra familia y que llevamos a la pareja sin saberlo.
Cuando dos sets de lealtades chocan, el conflicto parece irracional porque cada uno está siendo fiel a una lógica invisible.
El problema central de una pareja no es la comunicación: es la diferencia
El problema central de una pareja no es la comunicación: es la diferencia. Dos personas distintas, con historias distintas, intentando
construir una vida juntas. La clave no está en convencer al otro de que cambie, ni en encontrar a alguien que ya sea como tú quieres.
Está en aprender a navegar la diferencia con curiosidad en lugar de con hostilidad: ¿por qué mi pareja ve esto de forma tan distinta a
mí? ¿Qué dice eso de su historia, y de la mía?
Concepto integrado 37
5. Para abrir a alguien emocionalmente: empezar por el cuerpo. Antes de pedir que el otro ponga en palabras lo que siente,
preguntarle qué nota en su cuerpo. '¿Sientes tensión en algún sitio? ¿Hay algo que se activa físicamente cuando hablamos de esto?'
El cuerpo es la puerta de entrada al mundo emocional para muchas personas.
Concepto integrado 38
1. Cambiar la pregunta del conflicto. En lugar de '¿por qué hace esto?', preguntarse: '¿qué historia hay detrás de que mi pareja lo vea
así? ¿Y qué historia hay detrás de que a mí me afecte tanto?' La curiosidad abre; la acusación cierra.
Concepto integrado 39
2. Hacer visible lo invisible: las lealtades de origen. Identificar qué patrones heredaste de tu familia en áreas como dinero, afecto,
roles, conflicto. Compartirlos con tu pareja no como excusa sino como información. El contexto cambia cómo se interpretan los
comportamientos.
Concepto integrado 40
Amor condicional vs. amor que acepta: 'te amaré cuando cambies' es rechazo disfrazado de amor. Produce rigidez, no
transformación. El cambio real ocurre cuando alguien se siente aceptado tal como es y ve espacio para evolucionar.
Concepto integrado 41



--- PAGINA 26 ---

Manual maestro AAQ - fuentes originales - v2 editorial
26
El deseo de ser cambiado también juega un papel: cuando alguien dice 'te amaré si cambias', está comunicando rechazo. Y el
rechazo cierra, no abre. La persona que se siente rechazada se vuelve más rígida, no más flexible.
Concepto integrado 42
3. Abandonar el caso contra el otro. Cuando estás construyendo el caso contra tu pareja -reuniendo evidencias de todo lo que hace
mal- ya no puedes ver nada más. Practica preguntarte: ¿qué parte de esta dinámica soy yo también?
Concepto integrado 43
6. Ampliar el vocabulario emocional. Hay personas que solo tienen acceso a 'bien', 'mal' y 'normal'. Ofrecer palabras: '¿Podría ser que
sintieras frustración? ¿O quizás vergüenza?' No para ponerlas en la boca del otro, sino para expandir el mapa.
Concepto integrado 44
El cierre prematuro de la etiqueta: términos como 'narcisista', 'gaslighting' o 'love bombing' son útiles para nombrar experiencias, pero
si se usan como diagnóstico final detienen la investigación. El trabajo real empieza cuando la etiqueta se convierte en una pregunta,
no en una respuesta.
Concepto integrado 45
El juego de la culpa tiene una función defensiva: si el problema es el otro, no tengo que mirarme a mí. Pero mientras estoy ocupada
construyendo el caso contra mi pareja, no me estoy preguntando: ¿qué parte de esta dinámica soy yo? ¿Qué me activa a mí aquí, y
por qué?
La otredad (otherness): el otro en una relación es genuinamente diferente a ti
La otredad (otherness): el otro en una relación es genuinamente diferente a ti. No es una versión imperfecta de ti. Es una persona con
una historia distinta, lealtades distintas, y una forma de ver el mundo distinta. Navegar esa diferencia es el trabajo central de la pareja.
El sistema político de la pareja: cómo una pareja resuelve sus diferencias
El sistema político de la pareja: cómo una pareja resuelve sus diferencias. ¿Quién tiene más poder? ¿Se vota, se negocia, se evita,
se impone? El sistema puede ser democrático o autocrático. Hacerlo explícito es el primer paso para cambiarlo.
Además, somos narcisistas relacionales: solo vemos nuestro propio esfuerzo
Además, somos narcisistas relacionales: solo vemos nuestro propio esfuerzo. Solo percibimos el trabajo que nosotros hacemos en la
relación, no el del otro. Esto crea una asimetría crónica: cada uno siente que da más de lo que recibe.
Concepto integrado 49
La etiqueta (narcisista, gaslighter, love bomber) da una sensación de cierre que resulta muy alivianante. Pero también detiene la
investigación. Una vez que 'el otro es el problema', deja de haber preguntas. Y sin preguntas, no hay movimiento.
Concepto integrado 50
Narcisismo relacional: la tendencia humana a percibir solo el propio esfuerzo en la relación, sin ver el del otro. No es maldad: es un
sesgo cognitivo. Pero crea una asimetría crónica donde cada uno siente que da más.

Practica y criterios de aplicacion
Antes de actuar, separar hecho, interpretacion, emocion, necesidad y peticion. Esta distincion evita convertir activacion en mandato.
Buscar patrones repetidos, no momentos aislados. Un comportamiento puede tener significados distintos segun frecuencia,
reparacion y contexto.
Usar el cuerpo como dato, no como juez final. La activacion informa, pero no siempre describe la realidad completa.
Cerrar el ciclo con una accion observable: conversacion, limite, reparacion, pausa, decision o practica escrita.



--- PAGINA 28 ---

Manual maestro AAQ - fuentes originales - v2 editorial
28
6. Comunicacion, necesidades, limites y compasion
Tesis del capitulo
Una relacion madura necesita dos capacidades a la vez: apertura compasiva y limite claro. Sin compasion, el limite se vuelve castigo;
sin limite, la compasion se vuelve autoabandono.
Origen y logica del fenomeno
Muchas personas aprendieron que pedir era demasiado, que poner limites era rechazo o que amar significaba anticipar necesidades
sin nombrarlas.
Mecanismo central
El mecanismo se entiende como una cadena: experiencia previa, lectura de amenaza, respuesta corporal, estrategia protectora,
consecuencia relacional y confirmacion del mapa interno. El manual conserva esta cadena para que cada concepto pueda leerse
desde el origen hasta la solucion, no como consejo aislado.
Como se identifica
Se identifica observando senales repetidas: intensidad desproporcionada, retirada, protesta, confusion, necesidad no nombrada,
dificultad para reparar, eleccion de parejas no disponibles o perdida de agencia. La senal aislada importa menos que el patron y su
repeticion en contexto.
Errores frecuentes y excepciones
El error comun es moralizar el patron: llamar manipulacion a toda protesta, frialdad a toda necesidad de espacio o madurez a la
desconexion. La excepcion editorial es importante: una misma conducta puede ser defensa, limite sano o incompatibilidad real segun
contexto, historia y capacidad de reparacion.
Solucion practica
La practica central es hablar en primera persona: necesidad, emocion, peticion, limite y consecuencia. El otro no debe adivinar; debe
poder responder.
Fuentes integradas
Este capitulo integra 9 fuentes originales. La trazabilidad detallada queda fuera del PDF en el CSV v2.

because they have the same the same core and the same reasoning that it forms but different experiences entirely.
Concepto integrado 5
Distingue dos perfiles: desorganizado “callado” (se borra, cuida a todos, se vuelve invisible) y “ruidoso” (culpa a los demás en el
momento y luego se odia a sí mismo). Desarrolla el concepto de autoabandono y su efecto corporal: un ciclo de estrés crónico donde
el cerebro aprende que la seguridad viene de gestionar las emociones ajenas (codependencia), con cortisol alto y serotonina y
oxitocina bajas. Critica el discurso de “llama gemela/alma gemela” como señal de un sistema nervioso intoxicado y desregulado, no
de la pareja ideal. Cierra con promoción de su intensivo presencial (Relationship Mastery Intensive, San Diego, 23-26 abril 2026).

Manual maestro AAQ - fuentes originales - v2 editorial
29
Concepto integrado 6
En sesión, la persona dice cosas como “no importa que mis necesidades no se cubran, no importa que tenga miedo, lo aparto”. Justo
antes de disociarse, el autor la frena: “¿por qué no importa? ¿por qué tu seguridad no importa?”. La persona se congela: no puede
articularlo. Viene del lado derecho del cerebro, la zona emocional e implícita, anterior al lenguaje. Es una idea formada a los 2 años:
“mis necesidades no importan y a nadie le importaré nunca; pedir un abrazo o consuelo solo me trae un golpe o un grito, así que
mejor no pido”. Por eso el lado emocional, sin lenguaje, se congela: no sabe explicar por qué no importa.
Concepto integrado 7
El cuerpo funciona con un ciclo de estrés que no debía ser permanente, pero lo es. Al aprender a poner las necesidades de los
demás por delante, el sistema nervioso empieza a tratar las emociones ajenas como emergencias: alguien se molesta y el cortisol y la
adrenalina se disparan; todo el sistema se activa para arreglarlo. Cuando lo arreglas y el otro se calma, recibes un golpe de alivio:
dopamina más bajada de cortisol. El cerebro dice: “bien, estoy a salvo, había una emergencia, la resolví, recompensa”.
Concepto integrado 8
Nobody wakes up one day and decides to destroy their relationship, except people who have disorganized attachment style. Now,
none of the things I'm about to share with you in this video are going to be easy to hear, but they're things you must hear. Most people
who come to me with disorganized attachment style don't walk in with years of insight. They come in confused. They know something
is wrong. They can feel the pattern, but they can't name it.
Because at least rationalizing it is possible
Because at least rationalizing it is possible. If I ruin it when I'm trying, I will have to give up. That will hurt too much to live with. Your
system will lie to you and say, "I should run away from a good thing because something bad is about to happen." And you will ruin the
best relationships. Now, you're going to need objective external help. Somebody who can check in and tell you when you are being
crazy. Someone you can reality test with. Your brain will lie to you and you won't know what's happening.
Concepto integrado 10
They think other people are happy that they don't have a burden of caring for you. So, you try to be invisible. You assume people have
a negative opinion of you. I'm going to tell you this. You are wrong. You genuinely don't think anyone notices you. You try to be
invisible. You assume no one would respond kindly if you even asked for something. You think people have a negative opinion or no
opinion at all. That's your system lying to you. That's your system robbing you of the joy of human relationships and robbing the people
who love you of your presence and you think you're doing a good thing.
Concepto integrado 11
Well, here's what happens in sessions I have with coaching clients that go through this. They'll say something like, "Well, I I just I can't
worry about this problem." Or, you know, it just doesn't matter. It doesn't matter that my needs aren't getting met. It doesn't matter that
I don't feel good about this. It doesn't matter that I'm scared. It doesn't matter whatever. I'm just going to sweep it aside. And I see that
moment where they're about to dissociate from it.
Taking care of you will at least stop the pain
Taking care of you will at least stop the pain. This is when they usually start tearing up at this point. They usually have to wipe their
eyes. They pretend they're not just, oh, allergies, but they don't open up and just dump their feelings. It's deeper than that. That feeling
underneath is I can't care about myself or something bad will happen. That's the core belief running the whole thing. So, what is
actually happening in your body at this moment when you chronically disconnect from your needs over and over and over?
Concepto integrado 13
Maybe you're watching this so far and saying, "Yeah, Adam might do that, but so what?" Right? It's not that big a deal, right? It's it's not
that big a deal. I can just I can just keep rotating relationships every 5 years. Oh, who cares? It's not that big. No. Your body runs on a
stress cycle that was never supposed to be permanent for you, but it is permanent now. At least it has been so far. When you learn to
put everyone else's needs before your own, your nervous system adapted and it started treating other people's emotions as
emergencies.
La persona con apego desorganizado se ve a sí misma destruir relaciones buenas
La persona con apego desorganizado se ve a sí misma destruir relaciones buenas. Suele llegar confundida: sabe que algo va mal,
siente el patrón, pero no sabe nombrarlo. Se cierra justo cuando alguien se acerca, repite las mismas situaciones con personas
distintas y está agotada. Hay dos versiones: los “callados”, que desaparecen hacia dentro, y los “ruidosos”, que lo incendian todo. El
punto de cambio es siempre el mismo: dejar de señalar hacia fuera y mirar hacia dentro.
Concepto integrado 15
El desorganizado ruidoso, en el momento, culpa a los demás: cuando algo sale mal debe ser culpa de ellos, no estuvieron atentos, te
activaron. Pero cuando bajan las defensas y el otro no está delante para pelear, te giras contra ti: “me equivoqué, siempre es culpa
mía”. Te abandonas haciendo tuyos los problemas en el momento, hiriendo a quien quieres, y luego sin permitirte la dignidad de ser
un humano que sufre: te odias, te desprecias, desapareces cuando estás solo porque no sabes quién eres.
Concepto integrado 16
Qué es en realidad: estás tan estimulado e inundado de dopamina y de oxitocina al principio de la relación que no lo puedes creer. El
sistema está intoxicado con esa sensación: “eres todo lo que siempre quise, nunca me sentí así”. Pero ya te has sentido así otras



--- PAGINA 30 ---

Manual maestro AAQ - fuentes originales - v2 editorial
30
veces, con las personas anteriores que ahora no recuerdas porque sería incómodo recordarlas. El apego desorganizado en particular
está diseñado para apagar sentimientos, pensamientos y experiencias previas, y decir “este es mi verdadero compañero” una y otra
vez.
La calma se siente como un infierno
La calma se siente como un infierno. Cuando alguien es tranquilo, amable y gentil, tienes miedo, porque esperas que se transforme
en alguien horrible y te haga daño, o que tú lo arruines todo. Ambas cosas son un dolor que anticipas. El sistema dice: “aquí hay algo
malo, tengo que salir, mejor destruirlo yo porque dolerá menos si lo arruino a propósito”. Así arruinas las mejores relaciones.
Concepto integrado 18
Para los desorganizados ruidosos: quedarte en los momentos en que alguien te quiere y podrías ser vulnerable. Dejar de huir cuando
la cosa se vuelve real. Es un proceso que da miedo, no necesariamente doloroso si tienes una buena pareja. La anticipación del dolor
casi siempre es peor que la realidad.
Nada de lo que te pasó es culpa tuya
Nada de lo que te pasó es culpa tuya. Alguien te hizo cosas -intencionadas o no- que te hirieron y te asustaron. Pero la única persona
que puede cambiar cómo reaccionas y te comportas eres tú. No es justo: la carga es mayor de lo que debería, no debería haber
carga, las personas a cargo de criarte con seguridad te fallaron, por ignorancia o por desinterés. Aun así, nadie puede hacer este
trabajo por ti ni enfrentar tus miedos por ti.
Concepto integrado 20
Para los callados: retienes, no compartes nada, y te preguntas por qué nadie conecta contigo. Es porque envías el mensaje “no me
interesas, aléjate, no confío en ti”. Conviertes al otro en un caso de caridad y le dices que no es digno de tu tiempo. No te das cuenta
del daño que haces al retener el amor; y retener no es no dar, es bloquear la capacidad del otro de darte amor a ti. Las mejores
personas se sienten heridas y solas por tu sobreadaptación.
Concepto integrado 21
Imagen del desorganizado callado: la mujer que cocina y sirve tanto a todos que no se permite comer en todo el día; cuando los
demás se acuestan, agotada, se permite las sobras. Come lo justo para sobrevivir, se duerme y al día siguiente lo repite, invisible y
sin una queja. No pides nada; asumes que a nadie le importaría si lo hicieras. Te abandonas desapareciendo, sin permitirte ni sentir el
hambre.
Hombres y mujeres suelen ser parecidos; cambia el área donde se hiperfijan
Hombres y mujeres suelen ser parecidos; cambia el área donde se hiperfijan. Las mujeres tienden a autoabandonarse en lo relacional
y emocional: cuidan sin fin a los demás. Los hombres tienden a autoabandonarse en lo financiero o físico: dan sin fin y trabajan horas
interminables sin reconocimiento. Una mujer no suele empezar turnos de 16 horas para pagar la adicción al juego de otro; un hombre
puede que sí. Mismo patrón, distintos terrenos.
Concepto integrado 23
El problema: esa inundación al inicio (love bombing) lleva luego a una destrucción casi inevitable. Te metes tan hondo en el vínculo
que no quieres perderlo y harás lo que sea para retenerlo (“por favor no me dejes, por favor no me hagas daño, pero si lo haces,
supongo que no puedo evitarlo”). La intensidad es prueba de que tu sistema nervioso está desregulado, no de que sea la pareja
definitiva.
Concepto integrado 24
Smith expone cinco verdades duras que da a sus clientes con apego desorganizado: (1) el sistema nervioso miente y no puedes
detectarlo solo, la calma se vive como amenaza y empujas a sabotear lo bueno; (2) lo que más te aterra es justo lo que tienes que
hacer (hacerte visible si eres del tipo callado, quedarte en la intimidad si eres del tipo explosivo); (3) nada de lo que te pasó es culpa
tuya, pero solo tú puedes cambiarlo, aunque no sea justo; (4) los demás te importan más de lo que crees; (5) tus conductas, no tu
identidad ni tu daño, son el mayor problema en tus relaciones.
So, they watch themselves blow up good relationships
So, they watch themselves blow up good relationships. They watch themselves shut down right when someone gets close. They don't

They're still chaotic. They're still watching themselves destroy the very thing they want most. So, I tell them the truth, the things that
nobody else is willing to say to them. And it's what I'm going to share with you today.
Truth number one, your nervous system is lying to you
Truth number one, your nervous system is lying to you. And without proper training, you are not going to be able to figure out when

Concepto integrado 33
On the opposite end are people who bring the focus only onto their own experience: “I feel upset by this, so I need to cut you off -
you're a bad person.” They're quick to set extreme boundaries - concrete walls that push people away, rather than boundaries that
inform the dynamic and tell the other person how you want to be related to. This isn't heartlessness; it's protective. Pushing people
away too quickly is often a way of avoiding closeness, discomfort, conflict, difficult conversations, and being seen. You often see this in
people with an avoidant attachment style: there's such a lack of safety in connection, and conflict isn't experienced as safe in any
capacity, so they avoid it. This is problematic because it comes with a tendency to be quite judgemental - with ourselves and others -
and to easily project our fears. We have to find the centre between the two ends.

So, again, pressure tends to be one of those feelings that is totally quintessential to the avoidant experience. So, it's one that tends to

Concepto integrado 56
Okay? Because I wasn't making time or space for those things. At least not until my work was done. And the way that I set up my life,

those freedom fantasies sneak in to give us those kind of mental micro doses of those feelings or those nervous system states that

Concepto integrado 57
And the cool thing is that once we start seeing that pattern, so when we can start to recognize that pressure is actually a signal that
some very important part of us is being sectioned off from our experience in the relationship, then we can start looking at that pressure
as a clue about what deeper feelings might be wanting to come online for us or which ones might need to have more room made for
them inside of the relationship in order for it to become comfortable and sustainable in a long-term way.
Concepto integrado 58
So, whenever I find myself feeling a lot of pressure, either in a relationship or at work or whatever it is, I like to just ask myself the

I'm feeling kind of sad and I'm pressuring myself to get upbeat and happy again so that I can film, I might ask myself the question, what
if I just filmed this video from a bit of a sad or downregulated energy today?
Concepto integrado 59
So, another way of saying that is that relational needs are agreements that we make with another person about how we can stay in
connection across a long period of time while preserving each person's sense of wholeness. And to figure out what our specific
relational needs are, all we really have to do is look at where we naturally disconnect from connection and why. So, if you tend to avoid
people when you're feeling depressed, being seen and known in your depression is a relational need for you.
Concepto integrado 60
Maybe we're not trying to preserve a connection with our boss or our coworker where we bring in every single thing that we're feeling
during the workday. But when it comes to those deep intimate connections that we want to keep with us throughout different seasons
of our lives, this is the kind of stuff that it becomes really important to be able to let other people in on. Relational needs are about how
the relationship needs to expand and become big enough to include the full nervous system range of both people.
Concepto integrado 61
And that often takes some creativity to be able to get right, especially to be able to get it right over and over and over again. And so
this is why I wanted to spend so much time on recognizing and getting in touch with our feelings in this video because experiencing the
full range of our feelings is what allows us to stay regulated. It's what keeps all of our nervous systems in homeostasis. And our
relational needs are just the conditions under which our system can stay both regulated and connected to another person at the same
time so that we don't have to automatically disconnect every time we encounter one of those states that we think is incompatible with
connection.
Concepto integrado 62
Lo que sí llega a la conciencia son los efectos de segundo orden de ignorar esas señales. Normalmente, cuando el cuerpo registra
necesidad de consuelo, descanso o conexión, manda señales que producen pensamientos como “llama a un amigo y cuéntale, ve a
por un abrazo, tómate la tarde libre” (igual que el hambre hace pensar en pizza o ensalada). Pero si la mente aprendió a omitir esas
necesidades, lo que aparece en su lugar son pensamientos sobre lo que el evitativo asocia con la conexión: “ordena tu vida, haz algo
bien, sé útil a alguien o a un grupo”. Porque aprendió que ese es el estado desde el que le está permitido conectar.

Lo importante: cuando surge la fantasía, fíjate en cómo se siente en el cuerpo. ¿Es un gran exhalar donde se suelta toda la tensión?
